JAH Pragmatics Librarian answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PragmaticsTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

Speech Acts JAH-PRAG-000001 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

A speech act is an utterance that performs an action, not merely describes the world: saying 'I promise' makes a promise, 'I name this ship' names it, and 'I apologise' apologises. J. L. Austin distinguished the locutionary act of saying words, the illocutionary act of what is done in saying them, and the perlocutionary act of the effect on the hearer. Speech act theory explains how words can marry, sentence, order, and warn. In practice this means working through real examples such as I promise, I apologise, I name this ship, I warn you: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Speech act theory was founded by J. L. Austin in his 1955 William James lectures, published as How to Do Things with Words (1962), and developed by John Searle, who classified speech acts and stated their felicity conditions. It became a foundation of pragmatics and of the philosophy of language. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

A speech act is an utterance that performs an action, not merely describes the world: saying 'I promise' makes a promise, 'I name this ship' names it, and 'I apologise' apologises. J. L. Austin distinguished the locutionary act of saying words, the illocutionary act of what is done in saying them, and the perlocutionary act of the effect on the hearer. Speech act theory explains how words can marry, sentence, order, and warn. In practice this means working through real examples such as I promise, I apologise, I name this ship, I warn you: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

The Problem

What exactly is speech acts, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'utterances that do things': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples I promise, I apologise, I name this ship as its working material.

The Worked Solution

The Answer

The answer is the definition and analysis given in the description and method, applied to the examples above. The formal statement is saying 'I promise' = promising, the gloss is: saying is the utterance; 'I promise' is the example; promising is the act performed. The checkable counts are {'acts_listed': 6, 'austin_levels': 3, 'example_utterances': 6, 'searle_classes': 5}, and the demo recomputes them from the example list, so a reader who runs the demo obtains the same numbers. Limits

This explanation does not apply everywhere. Speech Acts has edge cases and disputed analyses, and different schools describe it in different terms; the history above names the main traditions, and a reader should compare them. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["I promise", "I apologise", "I name this ship", "I warn you", "I order you", "I bet"]
figures = {"acts_listed": 6, "austin_levels": 3, "example_utterances": 6, "searle_classes": 5}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class SpeechActsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["I promise", "I apologise", "I name this ship", "I warn you", "I order you", "I bet"]
        figures = {"acts_listed": 6, "austin_levels": 3, "example_utterances": 6, "searle_classes": 5}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SpeechActsProgram().run())

Gricean Implicature JAH-PRAG-000002 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

A Gricean implicature is a meaning that a speaker conveys without saying it directly, as when answering 'Do you like my cooking?' with 'Well, the presentation was lovely' implicates that the taste was not liked. Paul Grice explained implicature by the Cooperative Principle: hearers assume speakers are cooperative and work out what must be meant for the utterance to make sense. Conversational implicature is cancellable, calculable, and distinct from what is literally said. In practice this means working through real examples such as The presentation was lovely, Some students passed, It is warm in here, Can you pass the salt?: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Grice presented the theory in his 1967 William James lectures, published as Studies in the Way of Words (1989). It became the central theory of implied meaning in pragmatics, developed by Levinson, Horn, and Sperber and Wilson, and it underlies modern work on relevance and on pragmatic inference. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

A Gricean implicature is a meaning that a speaker conveys without saying it directly, as when answering 'Do you like my cooking?' with 'Well, the presentation was lovely' implicates that the taste was not liked. This explanation does not apply everywhere. Gricean Implicature has edge cases and disputed analyses, and different schools describe it in different terms; the history above names the main traditions, and a reader should compare them. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["The presentation was lovely", "Some students passed", "It is warm in here", "Can you pass the salt?", "I have a lot of work", "She is clever"]
figures = {"maxims": 4, "example_utterances": 6, "implicatures": 3, "principles": 1}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class GriceanImplicatureProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["The presentation was lovely", "Some students passed", "It is warm in here", "Can you pass the salt?", "I have a lot of work", "She is clever"]
        figures = {"maxims": 4, "example_utterances": 6, "implicatures": 3, "principles": 1}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(GriceanImplicatureProgram().run())

Presupposition JAH-PRAG-000003 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

A presupposition is a background assumption that an utterance takes for granted, as in 'John stopped smoking', which presupposes that John used to smoke. Presuppositions survive negation and questioning: 'John did not stop smoking' still suggests he smoked before. They can be triggered by words such as 'stop', 'again', 'regret', and definite descriptions, and they must be shared or accommodated by the hearer. In practice this means working through real examples such as John stopped smoking, The king of France is bald, She regrets leaving, He did it again: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Presupposition was discussed by Frege and Russell in the philosophy of language and became a central topic in linguistics in the 1970s through the work of Stalnaker, Karttunen, and others. The projection problem, how presuppositions behave in complex sentences, remains a major research area. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

inputs = ["John stopped smoking", "The king of France is bald", "She regrets leaving", "He did it again", "My sister is here", "It was John who left"]
figures = {"triggers": 4, "example_sentences": 6, "tests": 2, "survival_contexts": 2}

    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"

h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")

Deixis JAH-PRAG-000004 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Deixis is the use of words whose meaning depends on the context of utterance, such as 'I', 'you', 'here', 'there', 'now', and 'then'. 'I am here now' means something different for every speaker, place, and time, and hearers must know who is speaking, where, and when to understand it. Deictic expressions cover person, place, time, discourse, and social relations. In practice this means working through real examples such as I, you, here, there: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Deixis was studied by Karl Buehler in his Theory of Language (1934) and became a core topic in pragmatics through the work of Lyons, Fillmore, and Levinson. It is central to the study of pronouns, demonstratives, and tense, and to the design of systems that must resolve who and where an utterance refers to. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

History

Deixis was studied by Karl Buehler in his Theory of Language (1934) and became a core topic in pragmatics through the work of Lyons, Fillmore, and Levinson. It is central to the study of pronouns, demonstratives, and tense, and to the design of systems that must resolve who and where an utterance refers to. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Problem

What exactly is deixis, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'words pointing to context': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples I, you, here as its working material.

Method

Step 1: state the definition. Deixis is the use of words whose meaning depends on the context of utterance, such as 'I', 'you', 'here', 'there', 'now', and 'then'. 'I am here now' means something different for every speaker, place, and time, and hearers must know who is speaking, where, and when to understand it. Deictic expressions cover person, place, time, discourse, and social relations. Step 2: collect the examples I, you, here, there, now, then and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule 'I' = speaker; 'here' = place of utterance to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'deictic_types': 5, 'example_words': 6, 'dimensions': 3, 'speakers': 2}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["I", "you", "here", "there", "now", "then"]
figures = {"deictic_types": 5, "example_words": 6, "dimensions": 3, "speakers": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["I", "you", "here", "there", "now", "then"]
        figures = {"deictic_types": 5, "example_words": 6, "dimensions": 3, "speakers": 2}

        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"

        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"

Politeness Theory JAH-PRAG-000005 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Politeness theory explains how speakers protect face, the public self-image that people want to maintain, by softening requests and criticisms. Saying 'Could you possibly close the window?' is more polite than 'Close the window' because it gives the hearer an out and shows respect for their freedom. Politeness strategies vary by culture, by power, by distance, and by how imposing the act is. In practice this means working through real examples such as Could you possibly close the window?, Close the window, Would you mind helping?, Please help: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Politeness theory was developed by Penelope Brown and Stephen Levinson in Politeness: Some Universals in Language Usage (1978, revised 1987), building on Erving Goffman's work on face. It has been tested and debated in many cultures, and it remains the most influential account of polite language use. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Conversational Maxims JAH-PRAG-000006 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Grice's conversational maxims are four rules that speakers are expected to follow: Quality (be truthful), Quantity (be as informative as required, no more), Relation (be relevant), and Manner (be clear, brief, and orderly). Speakers can flout a maxim to generate an implicature, as when an obviously irrelevant answer implicates a refusal. The maxims describe the rational basis of conversation, not a set of etiquette rules. In practice this means working through real examples such as Be truthful, Be informative, Be relevant, Be clear: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Grice introduced the maxims in his 1967 lectures on logic and conversation. They have been enormously influential, generating work on implicature, relevance theory, and neo-Gricean pragmatics, and they are still taught as the starting point for the study of conversation. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Grice's conversational maxims are four rules that speakers are expected to follow: Quality (be truthful), Quantity (be as informative as required, no more), Relation (be relevant), and Manner (be clear, brief, and orderly). Speakers can flout a maxim to generate an implicature, as when an obviously irrelevant answer implicates a refusal. The maxims describe the rational basis of conversation, not a set of etiquette rules. In practice this means working through real examples such as Be truthful, Be informative, Be relevant, Be clear: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The Worked Solution

When It Applies

The explanation is supported by the linguistic evidence in the cited sources: attested examples, corpus data where applicable, and the descriptive tradition summarised in the history above. Grice's conversational maxims are four rules that speakers are expected to follow: Quality (be truthful), Quantity (be as informative as required, no more), Relation (be relevant), and Manner (be clear, brief, and orderly). Speakers can flout a maxim to generate an implicature, as when an obviously irrelevant answer implicates a refusal. The maxims describe the rational basis of conversation, not a set of etiquette rules. The demo does not prove the linguistic claim by itself; it proves that the counts and the rule application in this record are computed correctly from the stated inputs, while the sources confirm the facts about the language itself.

Limits

This explanation does not apply everywhere. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["Be truthful", "Be informative", "Be relevant", "Be clear", "Do not lie", "Avoid obscurity"]
figures = {"maxims": 4, "example_rules": 6, "flouts": 2, "principles": 1}
def transform(x):
    return len(x.split())
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ConversationalMaximsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["Be truthful", "Be informative", "Be relevant", "Be clear", "Do not lie", "Avoid obscurity"]
        figures = {"maxims": 4, "example_rules": 6, "flouts": 2, "principles": 1}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x.split())
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ConversationalMaximsProgram().run())

Indirect Speech Acts JAH-PRAG-000007 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

An indirect speech act is performed by means of another act, as when 'Can you pass the salt?' is a request made in the form of a question, or 'It is cold in here' is a request to close the window. Hearers recognise the intended act from context, convention, and the literal act, and indirectness is a central politeness strategy in many languages. In practice this means working through real examples such as Can you pass the salt?, It is cold in here, Would you like some tea?, The door is open: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Indirect speech acts were analysed by Searle in 1975, who showed that they are understood through shared conventions and inference. They have been studied extensively in cross-cultural pragmatics, where the same form can count as a request in one culture and as a mere observation in another. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["Can you pass the salt?", "It is cold in here", "Would you like some tea?", "The door is open", "I am thirsty", "Could you help me?"]
figures = {"indirect_requests": 4, "example_utterances": 6, "forms": 2, "functions": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}

    print(IndirectSpeechActsProgram().run())

Discourse Markers JAH-PRAG-000008 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Discourse markers are words and phrases such as 'well', 'you know', 'so', 'actually', and 'I mean' that organise conversation, signal a speaker's attitude, mark a change of topic, or manage turn-taking. They carry little propositional content but much interactional work, and their use varies by age, dialect, and register. Corpus studies show they are among the most frequent words in spoken language. In practice this means working through real examples such as well, you know, so, actually: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Discourse markers were studied in conversation analysis and in the work of Deborah Schiffrin (1987), who showed that markers such as 'oh', 'well', and 'you know' have systematic discourse functions. Later corpus work quantified their frequency and their role in spoken interaction. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["well", "you know", "so", "actually", "I mean", "oh"]
figures = {"markers_listed": 6, "functions": 4, "example_markers": 6, "registers": 3}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class DiscourseMarkersProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["well", "you know", "so", "actually", "I mean", "oh"]
        figures = {"markers_listed": 6, "functions": 4, "example_markers": 6, "registers": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(DiscourseMarkersProgram().run())

Context JAH-PRAG-000009 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Context is the background against which an utterance is understood: who is speaking, to whom, where, when, what has been said, and what is mutually known. The same sentence 'It is cold' can be a statement, a complaint, or a request to close a window, depending on context. Pragmatics is in large part the study of how context fixes reference, resolves ambiguity, and supplies what is left unsaid. In practice this means working through real examples such as It is cold, I am here, That one, Now: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Context was a central concern of Malinowski's work on the context of situation and of Firth's linguistics in the 1930s and 1950s. Modern pragmatics distinguishes linguistic context, physical context, social context, and common ground, and formal models represent context as a set of shared assumptions updated by each utterance. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Context is the background against which an utterance is understood: who is speaking, to whom, where, when, what has been said, and what is mutually known. Run 2 SHA-256: 3c518510d6bd8f9407644c93f979162d18bd1a5cfe2c91a234fea4c0e4e002f2

Full working program

import hashlib
class ContextProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["It is cold", "I am here", "That one", "Now", "Later", "We should go"]
        figures = {"context_types": 4, "example_utterances": 6, "dimensions": 3, "updates": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ContextProgram().run())

Reference JAH-PRAG-000010 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Reference is the use of language to pick out things in the world, as when 'the cat' refers to a particular cat known to speaker and hearer. Referring expressions include proper names, definite descriptions, pronouns, and demonstratives, and successful reference depends on shared knowledge and on the hearer's ability to identify the intended referent. Reference can fail when the hearer cannot identify what is meant. In practice this means working through real examples such as the cat, John, she, that book: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Reference was a central topic in the philosophy of language from Frege and Russell onwards, and in linguistics through the study of definiteness, anaphora, and givenness. Psycholinguistic work has shown that speakers design referring expressions for their hearers, giving more information when the referent is new and less when it is already known. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

import hashlib
inputs = ["the cat", "John", "she", "that book", "my sister", "it"]
figures = {"expression_types": 4, "example_expressions": 6, "failures": 2, "shared_knowledge": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")

Speech Act Classification JAH-PRAG-000011 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Speech act classification sorts utterances into kinds by their function. Searle's five classes are representatives, which state how things are; directives, which try to get the hearer to do something; commissives, which commit the speaker to a future act; expressives, which express a psychological state; and declarations, which change the world by being said, such as naming or sentencing. In practice this means working through real examples such as I state, I order, I promise, I apologise: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Searle proposed his classification in 1975, refining Austin's earlier taxonomy. It has become the standard classification in pragmatics and in the study of language in law, in computing, and in cross-cultural communication, although scholars debate borderline cases and the universality of the classes. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Speech act classification sorts utterances into kinds by their function. Searle's five classes are representatives, which state how things are; directives, which try to get the hearer to do something; commissives, which commit the speaker to a future act; expressives, which express a psychological state; and declarations, which change the world by being said, such as naming or sentencing. In practice this means working through real examples such as I state, I order, I promise, I apologise: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Specification

  • care: Check every example against the definition before applying the rule; do not generalise from a single example.
  • dimensions: {'austin_levels': 3, 'borderline_cases': 2, 'example_acts': 6, 'searle_classes': 5}
  • materials: Example list and key figures in this record.

  • A2: One hundred worked examples of Searle's five kinds of speech act.

Key figures

  • austin_levels: 3
  • borderline_cases: 2
  • example_acts: 6

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["I state", "I order", "I promise", "I apologise", "I declare", "I warn"]
figures = {"searle_classes": 5, "example_acts": 6, "austin_levels": 3, "borderline_cases": 2}
def transform(x):
    return len(x.split())
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class SpeechActClassificationProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["I state", "I order", "I promise", "I apologise", "I declare", "I warn"]
        figures = {"searle_classes": 5, "example_acts": 6, "austin_levels": 3, "borderline_cases": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return len(x.split())
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(SpeechActClassificationProgram().run())

Relevance Theory JAH-PRAG-000012 · complex

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Relevance theory holds that hearers interpret utterances by seeking the most relevant interpretation, the one that gives the greatest cognitive effect for the least processing effort. A speaker who says 'It is late' may implicate 'We should leave', because that is the most relevant reading in context. The theory replaces Grice's maxims with a single principle of relevance governing both production and comprehension. In practice this means working through real examples such as It is late, The kettle is boiling, I am tired, It is raining: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Relevance theory was developed by Dan Sperber and Deirdre Wilson in Relevance: Communication and Cognition (1986, revised 1995). It has become a major alternative to Gricean pragmatics, applied to implicature, irony, metaphor, and the interpretation of discourse. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Relevance theory holds that hearers interpret utterances by seeking the most relevant interpretation, the one that gives the greatest cognitive effect for the least processing effort. inputs = ["It is late", "The kettle is boiling", "I am tired", "It is raining", "We have no milk", "She is here"]
figures = {"principles": 1, "example_utterances": 6, "effects": 2, "effort_factors": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct

Common Ground JAH-PRAG-000013 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Common ground is the set of assumptions that speaker and hearer mutually take to be shared, built up as a conversation proceeds. Each utterance adds to common ground, and speakers design what they say for what they believe is already in common ground. Misunderstanding often comes from a mistaken assumption about what is shared. In practice this means working through real examples such as We both know, As you know, You remember, We agreed: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Common ground was formalised by Robert Stalnaker in the 1970s and developed by Herbert Clark, who showed how speakers and hearers collaborate to establish it. It is central to models of dialogue, to the study of reference, and to the design of conversational systems. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["We both know", "As you know", "You remember", "We agreed", "As I said", "You know"]
figures = {"updates_per_turn": 1, "example_phrases": 6, "participants": 2, "assumptions": 3}
def transform(x):
    return len(x.split())
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class CommonGroundProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["We both know", "As you know", "You remember", "We agreed", "As I said", "You know"]
        figures = {"updates_per_turn": 1, "example_phrases": 6, "participants": 2, "assumptions": 3}
        def transform(x):

            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

    print(CommonGroundProgram().run())

Adjacency Pairs JAH-PRAG-000014 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

An adjacency pair is a pair of turns in which the first part makes the second relevant, as a question makes an answer relevant, a greeting makes a greeting relevant, and an invitation makes an acceptance or refusal relevant. The second part is conditionally relevant: its absence is noticeable and accountable. Adjacency pairs are the basic building blocks of conversational sequence. In practice this means working through real examples such as Hello - Hello, How are you? - Fine, Will you come? - Yes, Thanks - You are welcome: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Adjacency pairs were identified in conversation analysis by Harvey Sacks, Emanuel Schegloff, and Gail Jefferson in the 1970s. They remain a core concept in the analysis of talk-in-interaction, in institutional talk, and in the study of how speakers manage agreement and disagreement. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

An adjacency pair is a pair of turns in which the first part makes the second relevant, as a question makes an answer relevant, a greeting makes a greeting relevant, and an invitation makes an acceptance or refusal relevant. The second part is conditionally relevant: its absence is noticeable and accountable. Adjacency pairs are the basic building blocks of conversational sequence. import hashlib
inputs = ["Hello - Hello", "How are you? - Fine", "Will you come? - Yes", "Thanks - You are welcome", "Goodbye - Goodbye", "Sorry - It is okay"]
figures = {"pairs_listed": 6, "pair_types": 4, "example_pairs": 6, "preferred_seconds": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Turn-Taking JAH-PRAG-000015 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Turn-taking is the system by which speakers take turns in conversation, with one speaker at a time and minimal gap and overlap. Speakers signal the end of a turn by grammar, intonation, and gaze, and next speakers can self-select or be selected by the current speaker. The system is orderly across languages, although the length of gaps and the tolerance for overlap vary by culture. In practice this means working through real examples such as Hello, Yes, Go on, I see: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Turn-taking was described in the classic 1974 paper by Sacks, Schegloff, and Jefferson, 'A Simplest Systematics for the Organization of Turn-Taking for Conversation'. It is a founding result of conversation analysis and has been confirmed in many languages and settings. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Turn-taking is the system by which speakers take turns in conversation, with one speaker at a time and minimal gap and overlap. Speakers signal the end of a turn by grammar, intonation, and gaze, and next speakers can self-select or be selected by the current speaker. Turn-taking is the system by which speakers take turns in conversation, with one speaker at a time and minimal gap and overlap. Speakers signal the end of a turn by grammar, intonation, and gaze, and next speakers can self-select or be selected by the current speaker. Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["Hello", "Yes", "Go on", "I see", "Right", "Okay"]
figures = {"speakers_at_once": 1, "example_turns": 6, "gap_tolerance": 2, "selection_methods": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class TurnTakingProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["Hello", "Yes", "Go on", "I see", "Right", "Okay"]
        figures = {"speakers_at_once": 1, "example_turns": 6, "gap_tolerance": 2, "selection_methods": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(TurnTakingProgram().run())

Conversational Repair JAH-PRAG-000016 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Conversational repair is the set of practices speakers use to fix trouble in speaking, hearing, or understanding, as when a speaker says 'I mean' to correct themselves, or a hearer says 'What?' to ask for a repeat. Repair can be self-initiated or other-initiated, and it is a central mechanism by which conversation stays on track. In practice this means working through real examples such as I mean, What?, Sorry, I meant, Pardon?: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Repair was analysed in conversation analysis by Schegloff, Jefferson, and Sacks in 1977, who distinguished self-repair from other-repair and showed a preference for self-repair. It is now studied in language acquisition, in aphasia, and in the design of conversational systems. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Hedging JAH-PRAG-000017 · simple

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Hedging is the softening of a claim or request with words such as 'sort of', 'kind of', 'maybe', 'I think', and 'perhaps', as in 'I think it might be sort of late'. Hedges reduce the force of an utterance, protect the speaker from being wrong, and show politeness. They are frequent in academic writing, in negotiations, and in talk where certainty is risky. In practice this means working through real examples such as sort of, kind of, maybe, I think: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Hedging was studied by George Lakoff in 1972 and developed in the study of academic discourse by Ken Hyland, who showed that hedges are a systematic feature of scholarly writing. It is now a standard topic in pragmatics, in discourse analysis, and in the teaching of academic English. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Hedging is the softening of a claim or request with words such as 'sort of', 'kind of', 'maybe', 'I think', and 'perhaps', as in 'I think it might be sort of late'. Hedges reduce the force of an utterance, protect the speaker from being wrong, and show politeness. They are frequent in academic writing, in negotiations, and in talk where certainty is risky. In practice this means working through real examples such as sort of, kind of, maybe, I think: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

The Problem

What exactly is hedging, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'softening a claim or request': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples sort of, kind of, maybe as its working material.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: state the definition. Hedging is the softening of a claim or request with words such as 'sort of', 'kind of', 'maybe', 'I think', and 'perhaps', as in 'I think it might be sort of late'. Hedges reduce the force of an utterance, protect the speaker from being wrong, and show politeness. They are frequent in academic writing, in negotiations, and in talk where certainty is risky. Step 2: collect the examples sort of, kind of, maybe, I think, perhaps, possibly and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule 'I think' + claim = softened claim to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'hedges_listed': 6, 'functions': 3, 'example_hedges': 6, 'registers': 3}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

The Answer

The answer is the definition and analysis given in the description and method, applied to the examples above. The formal statement is 'I think' + claim = softened claim, the gloss is: 'I think' is the hedge; claim is the statement; softened claim is the result. History

Hedging was studied by George Lakoff in 1972 and developed in the study of academic discourse by Ken Hyland, who showed that hedges are a systematic feature of scholarly writing. It is now a standard topic in pragmatics, in discourse analysis, and in the teaching of academic English. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["sort of", "kind of", "maybe", "I think", "perhaps", "possibly"]
figures = {"hedges_listed": 6, "functions": 3, "example_hedges": 6, "registers": 3}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class HedgingProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["sort of", "kind of", "maybe", "I think", "perhaps", "possibly"]
        figures = {"hedges_listed": 6, "functions": 3, "example_hedges": 6, "registers": 3}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(HedgingProgram().run())

Irony JAH-PRAG-000018 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Irony is saying the opposite of what is meant, as when a speaker caught in heavy rain says 'Lovely weather we are having'. Hearers recognise irony from tone, context, and the obvious falsity of the literal meaning, and they infer the intended attitude, often mocking or critical. Irony is a test case for theories of implicature and relevance. In practice this means working through real examples such as Lovely weather, Great job, Wonderful, Brilliant: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Irony was discussed by Grice as a form of flouting the maxim of Quality and developed in relevance theory by Sperber and Wilson, who treated it as echoic use. Psycholinguistic experiments have shown that irony takes longer to process than literal language, especially for children and for second-language speakers. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

import hashlib
inputs = ["Lovely weather", "Great job", "Wonderful", "Brilliant", "Nice going", "Perfect"]
figures = {"example_utterances": 6, "theories": 2, "cues": 3, "functions": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(IronyProgram().run())

Metaphor in Pragmatics JAH-PRAG-000019 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Metaphor in pragmatics is the use of a word or phrase to describe something in terms of another, as in 'Time is money' or 'She is a lion in debate'. Hearers must work out which properties are intended and which are not, and the interpretation depends on context, on shared knowledge, and on the speaker's purpose. Metaphor is not merely decorative; it shapes how people think and argue. In practice this means working through real examples such as Time is money, She is a lion, Life is a journey, He is a rock: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Metaphor was treated by Grice as a conversational implicature and by relevance theory as a loose use of language. George Lakoff and Mark Johnson argued in Metaphors We Live By (1980) that metaphor is a central structure of thought, and corpus work has since shown how widespread metaphor is in everyday language. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.
