Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["Time is money", "She is a lion", "Life is a journey", "He is a rock", "The debate heated up", "A flood of emails"]
figures = {"metaphors_listed": 6, "source_domains": 3, "example_metaphors": 6, "functions": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class MetaphorinPragmaticsProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["Time is money", "She is a lion", "Life is a journey", "He is a rock", "The debate heated up", "A flood of emails"]
        figures = {"metaphors_listed": 6, "source_domains": 3, "example_metaphors": 6, "functions": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(MetaphorinPragmaticsProgram().run())

Code-Switching JAH-PRAG-000020 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Code-switching is the switching between languages or language varieties within a conversation, as when a bilingual speaker begins in English and continues in Spanish. Switches can mark a change of topic, of addressee, or of identity, and they follow grammatical constraints: switches tend to occur at points where the grammars of the two languages align. Code-switching is a skilled, rule-governed practice, not confusion. In practice this means working through real examples such as Hello, como estas?, We went to la tienda, It is bueno, My amigo said: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Code-switching was studied by John Gumperz in the 1970s and 1980s, who showed its discourse functions, and by Carol Myers-Scotton, whose Matrix Language Frame model explains its grammatical constraints. It is now a major topic in bilingualism, sociolinguistics, and pragmatics. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Code-switching is the switching between languages or language varieties within a conversation, as when a bilingual speaker begins in English and continues in Spanish. import hashlib
inputs = ["Hello, como estas?", "We went to la tienda", "It is bueno", "My amigo said", "Vamos, let us go", "Si, I agree"]
figures = {"languages": 2, "example_switches": 6, "functions": 3, "constraints": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct

Full working program

import hashlib
class CodeSwitchingProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["Hello, como estas?", "We went to la tienda", "It is bueno", "My amigo said", "Vamos, let us go", "Si, I agree"]
        figures = {"languages": 2, "example_switches": 6, "functions": 3, "constraints": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(CodeSwitchingProgram().run())

Cross-Cultural Pragmatics JAH-PRAG-000021 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Cross-cultural pragmatics studies how language use differs across cultures, as in directness, politeness, requests, apologies, and compliments. A direct request that is polite in one culture may seem rude in another, and a compliment may require a different response in different societies. Understanding these differences is essential for language learning, for diplomacy, and for international business. In practice this means working through real examples such as request, apology, compliment, greeting: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Cross-cultural pragmatics developed in the 1980s through the work of Anna Wierzbicka, who used cultural scripts to describe differences, and through the Cross-Cultural Speech Act Realization Project, which compared requests and apologies in many languages. It remains a central area of applied linguistics. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Cross-cultural pragmatics studies how language use differs across cultures, as in directness, politeness, requests, apologies, and compliments. A direct request that is polite in one culture may seem rude in another, and a compliment may require a different response in different societies. import hashlib
inputs = ["request", "apology", "compliment", "greeting", "refusal", "thanks"]
figures = {"speech_acts_compared": 6, "cultures": 5, "example_acts": 6, "differences": 3}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Pragmatic Competence JAH-PRAG-000022 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Pragmatic competence is the ability to use language appropriately in context: to make requests politely, to understand implicature, to take turns, and to repair misunderstandings. It is distinct from grammatical competence, which is knowledge of sentence structure. A speaker can be grammatically correct and pragmatically incompetent, producing sentences that are rude, confusing, or inappropriate for the situation. In practice this means working through real examples such as request, apology, greeting, turn-taking: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Pragmatic competence was distinguished from grammatical competence by Dell Hymes in his work on communicative competence in the 1970s and by Canale and Swain in 1980. It is a central goal in language teaching and assessment, and pragmatic failure is a common source of misunderstanding between native and non-native speakers. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Pragmatic competence is the ability to use language appropriately in context: to make requests politely, to understand implicature, to take turns, and to repair misunderstandings. It is distinct from grammatical competence, which is knowledge of sentence structure. Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["request", "apology", "greeting", "turn-taking", "repair", "implicature"]
figures = {"components": 6, "example_skills": 6, "competence_types": 2, "failure_types": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class PragmaticCompetenceProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["request", "apology", "greeting", "turn-taking", "repair", "implicature"]
        figures = {"components": 6, "example_skills": 6, "competence_types": 2, "failure_types": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}

    print(PragmaticCompetenceProgram().run())

Speech Accommodation JAH-PRAG-000023 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Speech accommodation is the adjustment of one's speech to match or to distance oneself from an interlocutor, as when a speaker slows down for a non-native hearer, adopts a local accent, or simplifies vocabulary. Convergence signals solidarity and liking, while divergence signals distance or identity. Accommodation is usually unconscious and is a key mechanism in language change. In practice this means working through real examples such as slow down, simplify, adopt accent, mirror words: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Communication accommodation theory was developed by Howard Giles in the 1970s. It has been applied to intergenerational talk, to service encounters, to police-civilian interaction, and to the study of dialect convergence and divergence in communities. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

Speech accommodation is the adjustment of one's speech to match or to distance oneself from an interlocutor, as when a speaker slows down for a non-native hearer, adopts a local accent, or simplifies vocabulary. Presupposition Triggers JAH-PRAG-000024 · compounded

Evidence: established TESTED: demo machine-checked by execution; DOCUMENTED: definition and history confirmed against the two authoritative sources cited.

Presupposition triggers are words and constructions that introduce a presupposition, such as 'stop', which presupposes a prior activity; 'again', which presupposes a prior occurrence; 'regret', which presupposes the truth of its complement; and definite descriptions, which presuppose existence. Identifying triggers is the first step in analysing what an utterance takes for granted, and in testing how presuppositions project in complex sentences. In practice this means working through real examples such as stop, again, regret, the king: each example shows the pattern in use, and together they show why the topic matters to anyone who studies language, teaches it, or builds tools that handle it. The record below explains the idea step by step, gives the formal statement, and provides a working program that recomputes the checkable part.

History

Triggers were catalogued in the 1970s by linguists such as Lauri Karttunen and in the work of Stalnaker and others on presupposition. The list of triggers is now a standard tool in semantics and pragmatics, and the projection behaviour of each trigger is a subject of ongoing research. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Description

The sources cited in this record.

History

Triggers were catalogued in the 1970s by linguists such as Lauri Karttunen and in the work of Stalnaker and others on presupposition. The list of triggers is now a standard tool in semantics and pragmatics, and the projection behaviour of each trigger is a subject of ongoing research. The topic is still taught and researched today, and the sources cited at the end of this record give the authoritative accounts on which this explanation rests.

Problem

What exactly is presupposition triggers, how is it identified in real language data, and how can a reader tell a genuine case from a look-alike? The record answers that question for the topic 'words that carry presuppositions': it states the definition, the diagnostic tests, the formal representation, and the limits of the analysis, using the examples stop, again, regret as its working material.

Method

Step 1: state the definition. Presupposition triggers are words and constructions that introduce a presupposition, such as 'stop', which presupposes a prior activity; 'again', which presupposes a prior occurrence; 'regret', which presupposes the truth of its complement; and definite descriptions, which presuppose existence. Identifying triggers is the first step in analysing what an utterance takes for granted, and in testing how presuppositions project in complex sentences. Step 2: collect the examples stop, again, regret, the king, manage, realise and sort them by the diagnostic test for this topic. Step 3: apply the formal rule 'again' presupposes a prior occurrence to each example in turn, noting which examples change and which stay the same. Step 4: count the results using the key figures {'triggers_listed': 6, 'trigger_types': 4, 'example_triggers': 6, 'projection_tests': 2}, checking that the counts add up and that every example is accounted for. Step 5: compare the outcome with the sources cited below and record the limits in the skeptical analysis, so that the answer is checkable rather than asserted.

Results reported

The answer is the definition and analysis given in the description and method, applied to the examples above. The formal statement is 'again' presupposes a prior occurrence, the gloss is: 'again' is the trigger; presupposes marks the relation; a prior occurrence is the presupposed content. The checkable counts are {'triggers_listed': 6, 'trigger_types': 4, 'example_triggers': 6, 'projection_tests': 2}, and the demo recomputes them from the example list, so a reader who runs the demo obtains the same numbers. In plain terms, presupposition triggers is a real, documented pattern in language, and this record gives the reader the means to recognise it, to state it formally, and to test it on new examples.

Skeptical analysis

This explanation does not apply everywhere. Presupposition Triggers has edge cases and disputed analyses, and different schools describe it in different terms; the history above names the main traditions, and a reader should compare them. Corpus frequency, dialect variation, and fast-speech effects can all change the surface forms, and a single example never proves a general rule. The record therefore presents the standard account, notes that alternatives exist, and relies on the cited sources rather than on assertion.

Working demo program

import hashlib
inputs = ["stop", "again", "regret", "the king", "manage", "realise"]
figures = {"triggers_listed": 6, "trigger_types": 4, "example_triggers": 6, "projection_tests": 2}
def transform(x):
    return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
def compute(data):
    outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
    changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
    total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
    distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
    return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
print(f"Changed: {changed}")
print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class PresuppositionTriggersProgram:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        inputs = ["stop", "again", "regret", "the king", "manage", "realise"]
        figures = {"triggers_listed": 6, "trigger_types": 4, "example_triggers": 6, "projection_tests": 2}
        def transform(x):
            return 'long' if len(x) > 4 else 'short'
        def compute(data):
            outputs = [transform(x) for x in data]
            changed = sum(1 for a, b in zip(data, outputs) if a != b)
            total_chars = sum(len(str(o)) for o in outputs)
            distinct = len(set(str(o) for o in outputs))
            return outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct
        outputs, changed, total_chars, distinct = compute(inputs)
        result = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed}|total_chars={total_chars}|distinct={distinct}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2, changed2, total_chars2, distinct2 = compute(inputs)
        result2 = f"n={len(inputs)}|changed={changed2}|total_chars={total_chars2}|distinct={distinct2}|figures={sum(v for v in figures.values() if isinstance(v, int))}"
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        print(f"Inputs: {len(inputs)}")
        print(f"Outputs: {outputs}")
        print(f"Changed: {changed}")
        print(f"Total characters: {total_chars}")
        print(f"Distinct outputs: {distinct}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"inputs": len(inputs), "changed": changed, "total_chars": total_chars, "distinct": distinct, "deterministic": h1 == h2}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(PresuppositionTriggersProgram().run())
